Document 002: 1-8月财政收支数据解读：8月财政收入同比增速有所回落

Source PDF: all report pdf/1-8月财政收支数据解读：8月财政收入同比增速有所回落.pdf
Report source: 国新证券股份
Date: 2026-10-02
Pages: 5
Status: ok

### Page 1

市场研究部 2026年9月21日
事件
9 月 18 日，财政部发布，1-8 月，全国一般公共预算收入
156633亿元，同比增长 5.7%；全国一般公共预算支出 181451亿
元，同比增长 1.2%。
解读
一、1-8 月财政收入增速比 1-7 月小幅回落，8 月财政收入、
地方一般公共预算本级收入和税收收入增速比7月有所回落。
1-8月，全国一般公共预算收入 156633亿元，同比增长 5.7%，
比 1-7月回落 0.1个百分点，比 2025年同期提高 5.4个百分点。
其中，8 月，全国一般公共预算收入 12937 亿元，同比增长
4.7%，比 7月回落 7.0 个百分点，比 2025 年同期提高 2.7 个百分
点。
1-8月，全国税收收入同比增长 6.6%，比 1-7月回落 0.1个百
分点，比 2025年同期提高 6.6个百分点；非税收入同比增长 1.5%，
比 1-7月回落 0.1个百分点，与 2025年同期持平。
其中，8 月全国税收收入同比增长 5.6%，比 7 月回落 8.2 个

百分点，比 2025 年同期提高 2.2 个百分点；非税收入同比增长
0.3%，比 7月提高 5.7 个百分点，比 2025 年同期提高 4.1 个百分
点。
分中央和地方看，1-8 月，中央一般公共预算收入同比增长
9.1%，与 1-7月持平；地方一般公共预算本级收入同比增长 3.1%，
比 1-7月回落 0.2个百分点。
8月，中央一般公共预算收入同比增长 9.3%，比 7月回落 7.5
个百分点；地方一般公共预算本级收入同比增长 0.7%，比 7 月回
落 6.1个百分点。
二、8 月国内增值税、国内消费税、企业所得税、出口退税、
个人所得税、证券交易印花税等主体税种增速均比7月回落。
1-8 月，国内增值税 50179 亿元，同比增长 5.9%，比 1-7 月
回落 0.2 个百分点。其中，8 月同比增长 4.0%， 比 7 月回落 2.7
个百分点。
宏观研究
8月财政收入同比增速有所回落
——1-8月财政收支数据解读
市场表现截至 2026.8.31

国内消费税 11111亿元，同比下降 3.6%，降幅比 1-7 月扩大
1.4 个百分点。 其中，8 月同比下降 14.0%， 比 7 月回落 20.3 个
百分点。
企业所得税 33786 亿元，同比增长 7.3%，比 1-7 月提高 0.1
个百分点。 其中，8月同比增长 11.6%， 比 7月回落 10.2 个百分
点。
个人所得税 12080亿元，同比增长 14.5%，比 1-7月回落 0.4
个百分点。 其中，8月同比增长 11.8%， 比 7月回落 14.1 个百分
点。
进口货物增值税、消费税 13258 亿元，同比增长 12.6%，比
1-7月提高 0.3 个百分点。其中，8月同比增长 14.9%， 比 7月回
落 0.4个百分点。
出口货物退增值税、消费税 16472亿元，同比增长 4.5%，比
1-7 月回落 0.7 个百分点。 其中，8 月同比下降 1.7%， 比 7 月回
落 5.4个百分点。
1-8 月，印花税 3858 亿元，同比增长 35.6%，比 1-7 月回落

2.4 个百分点。其中，8 月同比增长 15.1%， 比 7 月回落 13.3 个
百分点。
1-8 月，证券交易印花税 2160 亿元，同比增长 82%，比 1-7
月回落 17.2个百分点。 其中，8月同比增长 17.9%， 比 7月回落
90.7个百分点。
三、1-8 月全国财政支出小幅增长，比 1-7 月小幅回落，8 月
同比小幅下降。
1-8月，全国一般公共预算支出 181451亿元，同比增长 1.2%，
比 1-7月回落 0.1个百分点。
分中央和地方看，中央一般公共预算本级支出 28177 亿元，
同比增长 6%，比 1-7 月回落 0.2 个百分点；地方一般公共预算支
出 153274亿元，同比增长 0.3%，比 1-7月回落 0.2个百分点。
1-8月，财政支出增速较高的是，卫生健康支出同比增长 9.2%，
比 1-7月回落 0.6个百分点，社会保障支出同比增长 6.5%，比 1-7
月回落 0.5 个百分点，债务付息支出同比增长 5.4%，比 1-7 月提
高 0.2个百分点，科学技术支出同比增长 2.2%，比 1-7月提高 0.7

个百分点，教育支出同比增长 0.7%，与 1-7月持平。
其余支出 1-8 月均同比下降，其中，农林水支出同比下降
6.0%，节能环保支出同比下降 12.3%，文化旅游支出同比下降
7.2%，交通运输支出同比下降 2.9%，城乡社区事务支出同比下降
3.0%。
其中，8 月，全国一般公共预算支出同比下降 0.1%，比 7 月
回落 0.6 个百分点，其中，中央支出同比增长 4.7%，比 7 月提高

19.0%，降幅比 1-7 月收窄 2.2 个百分点。分中央和地方看，中央
政府性基金预算收入 3292亿元，同比增长 12.2%，比 1-7 月提高
5.4个百分点；地方政府性基金预算本级收入 18127亿元，同比下

个百分点。

收窄 15.4 个百分点；分中央和地方看，中央政府性基金预算收入

算本级收入同比下降 10.3%，降幅比 7月收窄 9.9个百分点，其中，

国有土地使用权出让收入同比下降 12.6%，降幅比 7 月收窄 14.5
个百分点。
1-8 月，全国政府性基金预算支出 51948 亿元，同比下降
17.0%，降幅比 1-7 月扩大 0.6 个百分点。分中央和地方看，中央
政府性基金预算本级支出 3175亿元，同比下降 58.3%，降幅比 1-
7月收窄 3.5个百分点；地方政府性基金预算支出 48773亿元，同
比下降 11.3%，降幅比 1-7 月扩大 1.8 个百分点，其中，国有土地
使用权出让收入相关支出 21786 亿元，同比下降 18.5%，降幅比
1-7月扩大 1.1个百分点。
8月，全国政府性基金预算支出同比下降 21.4%，降幅比 7月
扩大 5.0个百分点。分中央和地方看，中央政府性基金预算本级支
出同比下降 8.8%，降幅比 7月收窄 31.3个百分点；地方政府性基

国有土地使用权出让收入相关支出同比下降 26.8%，降幅比 7月扩
大 6.6个百分点。

五、预测9月财政收入同比增速小幅提高。
8月国内增值税、国内消费税、企业所得税、个人所得税等主
体税种增速均比 7月有所回落，税收收入增速小幅放缓。预测 9月
财政收入同比增速小幅提高。
投资建议
1、8月国内增值税、国内消费税、企业所得税、个人所得税等主
体税种增速比 7月明显回落；
2、8月证券交易印花税同比增速比 7月大幅回落。

2026 年 10 月 9 日 全9 頁

Indicators Update 
2026 年 8 月消費統計 
耐久財とサービスが弱く、総じて見れば前月から減少

経済調査部 エコノミスト 龐 鈞文

[要約] 
◼ 2026 年8 月の家計調査における二人以上世帯の実質消費支出は前月比＋0.1％と2 カ月
連続で増加した。 一方、複数の需要側統計を用いて補正した世帯消費動向指数（ CTI
ミクロ）で見た実質消費は同▲2.3％と 2 カ月ぶりに減少した。また、供給側統計の 1
つである商業動態統計では、CPI の財指数で実質化した小売販売額は同▲1.1％だった。
総じて見れば、8 月の個人消費は前月から減少したと判断される。 
◼ 先行きの個人消費は 底堅く推移するとみている 。名目賃金は春闘での高水準の賃上げ
や最低賃金の引き上げもあって上昇が続く見込みである。 一方、政府の物価高対策 の
効果の剝落などが物価を押し上げる要因となろう。加えて、原油の輸入価格は代替調
達もあって高止まりしており、幅広い分野の価格への波及を通じて個人消費を下押し
する可能性には引き続き注意が必要だ。

＜2026 年8 月の消費総括＞耐久財やサービスを中心に減少 
需要側統計である家計調査における二人以上世帯の実質消費支出は、前月比＋0.1％と2カ月
連続で小幅に増加した一方、複数の需要側統計を用いて補正した世帯消費動向指数（CTI ミク
ロ）で見た実質消費は同▲2.3％と 2 カ月ぶりに減少した（図表 1）。また、供給側統計の 1 つ
である商業動態統計では、CPI の財指数で実質化した小売販売額は同▲1.1％だった。総じて見
れば、2026年8月の個人消費は前月から減少したと判断される。

＜CTI ミクロ・家計調査（需要側）＞「交通・通信」などの7 費目が減少 
2026年 8 月の CTIミクロ（二人以上の世帯）を費目別に見ると、10 大費目1のうち、 「交通・
通信」 （前月比▲5.3％） 、「その他」 （同▲4.1％）、 「教養娯楽」 （同▲2.9％） 、 「教育」 （同▲
4.7％） 、「家具・家事用品」 （同▲4.4％） 、「被服及び履物」 （同▲3.7％） 、「保健医療」 （同▲
0.5％）の7費目が減少した。 
他方、「光熱・水道」 （前月比＋4.4％） 、「住居」 （同＋3.3％） 、「食料」 （同＋0.5％）の3費目
が増加した（図表2）。

算出している。

1

3 / 9

続いて、CTIミクロの10大費目に含まれる個別品目への支出について確認する。 
CTI ミクロにおける「交通・通信」は 2 カ月ぶりに減少した。自動車等関係費などへの支出
が減少した。「その他」は2カ月ぶりに減少した。諸雑費やこづかいなどへの支出が減少した。
「教養娯楽」は2カ月ぶりに減少した。教養娯楽サービスを中心に、教養娯楽用品などへの支出
が減少した。 「教育」は2 カ月連続で減少した。授業料等を中心に支出が減少した2。「家具・家
事用品」は2カ月ぶりに減少した。家庭用耐久財を中心に支出が減少した。「被服及び履物」は
2 カ月ぶりに減少した。洋服を中心に支出が減少した。「保健医療」は 2 カ月連続で減少した。
保健医療サービスを中心に支出が減少した。 
他方、「光熱・水道」は 2 カ月ぶりに増加した。電気代やガス代を中心に支出が拡大した。
「住居」は 2 カ月ぶりに増加した。家賃地代を中心に支出が拡大した。「食料」は 2 カ月連続で
増加した。酒類や野菜・海藻を中心に支出が拡大した。 
家計調査における二人以上世帯の実質消費支出は、前月比＋0.1％と2カ月連続で小幅に増加
した。ただし、振れの大きい項目を除いた、住居等を除くベースでは同▲1.7％と2カ月ぶりに
減少した（図表 3 上段左）。 
基礎的支出は前月比＋0.9％と、4 カ月ぶりに増加した。また、選択的支出は同＋1.3％と、2
カ月連続で増加した（いずれも大和総研による季節調整値、図表3 中段左）。 
消費支出を財・サービス別に見ると（大和総研による季節調整値、図表 3 中段右）、耐久財
（前月比＋8.2％）が4カ月ぶりに増加し、全体を押し上げた。非耐久財（同＋0.4％）も3カ月
ぶりに増加した。一方、サービス（同▲0.3％）と半耐久財（同▲0.1％）は減少した。

2 ただし、授業料等は児童や学生がいる一部の世帯以外はほとんど支出しないため、サンプルサイズが小さく
振れが大きい。

＜商業動態統計（供給側）＞小売販売額は名目・実質ともに2 カ月ぶりに減少 
2026 年 8 月の商業動態統計によると、名目小売販売額は前月比▲1.2％と 2 カ月ぶりに減少
した（図表 4、5） 。また、CPIの財指数で実質化した小売販売額は同▲1.1％だった。 
名目小売販売額を業種別に見ると、全ての業種が前月から減少した。「機械器具小売業」 （前
月比▲6.9％）は 2 カ月ぶりに減少した。「自動車小売業」 （同▲2.5％）は 4 カ月連続で減少し
た。8 月の新車販売台数（大和総研による季節調整値）は同▲7.6％と減少しており3、この結
果とも整合的だ。「燃料小売業」 （同▲1.8％）は2カ月ぶりに減少した。「飲食料品小売業」 （同
▲0.4％）は2カ月ぶりに減少した。コンビニの加工食品（同▲1.5％）が減少した。「織物・衣
服・身の回り品小売業」 （同▲1.8％）、「その他小売業」 （同▲0.2％）、「各種商品小売業」 （同
▲0.4％）はいずれも2カ月ぶりに減少した。

＜2026 年9 月の消費＞8 月から減少した可能性 
業界統計や個社データ、JCB 消費 NOW をもとに判断すると、2026 年 9 月の消費は 8 月から減
少したとみている4。財消費では、9 月前半の実績をもとに試算したスーパーの JCB 消費額（大
和総研による季節調整値）と家電の JCB 消費額（同）は減少した。一方、アパレルの売上高は
堅調に推移し、新車販売台数（同）も増加した。サービス消費では、宿泊の JCB 消費額（同）
と外食の JCB 消費額（同）は天候不順もあっていずれも前月から減少した。一方、新幹線輸送
量の前年比伸び率は前月から拡大した。

＜先行き＞個人消費は底堅く推移する見込み／価格上昇の広がりに注意 
先行きの個人消費は底堅く推移するとみている。名目賃金の伸びが高水準で推移する一方、
政府の物価高対策による物価の押し下げ効果の剝落などで、物価上昇率が高まり、実質賃金が
伸び悩む可能性がある。 
名目賃金の伸びは高水準で推移するだろう。春闘の結果5は前年をやや下回るものの、高水準
となった。また、 2026 年度の最低賃金の 引き上げ率は全国加重平均で＋ 5.0％（前年度は＋
6.3％）と高水準を維持した6。 
消費者物価について、7 月使用分から 9 月使用分まで実施される電気・ガス料金の補助とい
った政府の物価高対策が終了した。加えて、代替調達もあって原油の輸入価格は高止まりして
おり7、原材料費や輸送費の上昇を通じて幅広い分野の価格に波及し、消費を下押しする可能性
には注意が必要だ。帝国データバンクによると、値上げされた飲食料品のうち中東情勢の影響
を受けた品目の割合は 9 月末時点で約 3 割だった。今後も値上げが継続する見込みだが、値上
げの品目数は10月で一旦ピークアウトする可能性もある8。 
また、金利上昇による住宅ローン等の利払い負担増が個人消費にもたらす悪影響にも警戒す
る必要がある9。さらに、消費動向調査（内閣府）によると、9 月の消費者態度指数（二人以上
の世帯、季節調整値 ）は 35.4（前月差▲0.1pt）と小幅に低下し、依然として低水準にあり
（巻末図表「消費者マインド」参照） 、節約志向の高まりに注意が必要だ。

4 詳細は、拙稿「消費データブック（2026/10/6 号）」（大和総研レポート、2026 年10月 6 日）を参照。 
5 日本労働組合総連合会（連合）「3 年連続の 5％超え！～2026 春季生活闘争 第7 回（最終）回答集計結果
について～」（2026 年7 月3日）によると、定昇相当込みの賃上げ率の平均は 5.01％（前年同時期は
5.25％）となった。 
6 厚生労働省「地域別最低賃金の全国一覧」（2026 年10 月9 日閲覧） 
7 世界的な需給の引き締まりも受け、ホルムズ海峡を経由しないルートでの中東や他地域からの原油の代替調
達の価格が割高となり、調達コストが押し上げられている。詳細は、神田慶司・畑中宏仁・中村華奈子・横田
凱「日本経済見通し：2026 年8 月」（大和総研レポート、2026 年8 月 24 日）を参照。 
8 帝国データバンク「『食品主要 195 社』価格改定動向調査 ― 2026 年 10 月 飲食料品値上げ、10 月は3153
品目 値上げラッシュはピーク越え」（2026 年9 月30 日） 
9 詳細は、神田慶司・畑中宏仁・中村華奈子・横田凱「日本経済見通し：2026 年8 月」（大和総研レポート、
2026 年8 月24 日）を参照。

Document 037: 2026年8月雇用統計

Source PDF: all report pdf/2026年8月雇用統計.pdf
Report source: DIR
Date: 2026-10-02
Pages: 8
Status: ok

### Page 1

日本

株式会社大和総研 丸の内オフィス 〒100-6756 東京都千代田区丸の内一丁目 9 番 1 号 グラントウキョウ ノースタワー 
このレポートは投資勧誘を意図して提供するものではありません。このレポートの掲載情報は信頼できると考えられる情報源から作成しておりますが、その正確性、完全
性を保証するものではありません。また、記載された意見や予測等は作成時点のものであり今後予告なく変更されることがあります。㈱大和総研と大和証券㈱は、㈱大和
証券グループ本社を親会社とする大和証券グループの会社です。内容に関する一切の権利は㈱大和総研にあります。無断での複製・転載・転送等はご遠慮ください。

2026 年 10 月 2 日 全8 頁

Indicators Update 
2026 年 8 月雇用統計 
失業率は2.5％と前月から0.1％pt 上昇も、雇用環境は概ね横ばい

経済調査部 エコノミスト 菊池 慈陽

[要約] 
◼ 2026 年 8 月の完全失業率（季節調整値）は 2.5％と、5 カ月ぶりに上昇（前月差＋
0.1％pt）し、4～6 月の水準に戻った。失業者数は 2 カ月ぶりに増加（同＋10 万人）
したものの自発的な離職が中心で、就業者数は小幅ながら3 カ月ぶりに増加（同＋3 万
人）した。総じて見れば、雇用環境は前月から概ね横ばいで推移したとみられる。 
◼ 2026 年8 月の有効求人倍率（季節調整値）は1.18 倍と前月から横ばいだった。他方、
新規求人倍率は2.06 倍（前月差▲0.04pt）と前月から低下した。 
◼ 先行きの雇用環境は堅調に推移しよう。労働供給が中長期的に減少していく可能性が
高いこともあり、企業は高水準の賃上げなど、人材確保に向けた積極的な取り組みを
続けている。ただし、 雇用環境の悪化リスクは小さくない。中東情勢を めぐる不確実
性は依然として高く、長期化する原油高が企業収益を悪化させ、幅広い産業で労働需
要を下押しする恐れがある。

図表 1：雇用関連指標の推移

8 月の完全失業率：2.5％と前月から上昇も、失業者の増加は「自発的な離職」が中心 
2026 年 8 月の完全失業率（季節調整値）は2.5％と、5 カ月ぶりに上昇（前月差＋0.1％pt）
し、4～6 月の水準に戻った（図表 2 左上） 。失業者数は2 カ月ぶりに増加（同＋10 万人）し、
就業者数は小幅ながら 3 カ月ぶりに増加（同＋3 万人）した。総じて見れば、雇用環境は前月
から概ね横ばいで推移したとみられる。 
失業者数を求職理由別に見ると、「自発的な離職」 （前月差＋6 万人）が増加し、全体を押し
上げた。ほかにも「定年又は雇用契約の満了」は同＋2 万人、「勤め先や事業の都合による離職」
は同＋1 万人、「新たに求職」は同＋1 万人と、幅広く増加した（巻末の雇用概況①下段左）。 
就業者数の前月からの変化 を業種別に見ると、「学術研究，専門・技術サービス業 」など一
部の非製造業の増加が目立った。5、6 月に下振れしていた反動が表れたとみられる。「卸売業，
小売業」も増加した。他方、「製造業」、「建設業」、 「情報通信業」は減少した。「医療，福祉」
は概ね横ばいだった（図表 2 左下）。 
雇用者数（役員を除く）を雇用形態別に見ると、正規雇用者は前月から横ばいだった一方、
非正規雇用者は4 カ月連続で減少（前月差▲10 万人）した（図表2 右下）。 
図表 2：就業者数・完全失業率（左上） 、非労働力人口・労働参加率（右上） 、

8 月の新規求人倍率：求職が増加した一方で求人は減少し、前月から低下 
2026 年 8 月の有効求人倍率（季節調整値）は 1.18 倍と前月から横ばいだった。他方、新規
求人倍率は 2.06 倍（前月差▲0.04pt）と前月から低下した（図表3 左）。 
求人側の動きを見ると、有効求人数（前月比＋0.7％）は2 カ月ぶりに増加した一方、新規求
人数（同▲1.5％）は 3 カ月ぶりに減少した（図表 4 左） 。求職側では、有効求職者数（同＋
0.7％）、新規求職申込件数（同＋0.6％）はいずれも 2 カ月連続で増加した。求職側は 2025 年
春頃から持ち直しの動きが見られており、求人側も2026 年春頃から増加傾向にある。 
雇用形態別に見ると、正社員の有効求人倍率（季節調整値）は1.00 倍と3 カ月連続で同水準
だった。新規求人倍率は1.68 倍（前月差▲0.07pt）と2 カ月連続で低下した（図表3 右）。

先行き：堅調な推移を見込むも、中東情勢など雇用環境の悪化リスクは小さくない 
先行きの雇用環境は、堅調に推移することを見込んでいる。労働供給が中長期的に減少して
いく可能性が高いこともあり、企業は高水準の賃上げなど1、人材確保に向けた積極的な取り組
みを続けている。 
日本銀行「全国企業短期経済観測調査」 （日銀短観）の2026 年 9 月調査では、雇用人員判断
DI（全規模全産業、 「過剰」－「不足」 ）は「最近」が▲38％pt と 6 月調査から 1％pt 低下し
（人手不足感が強まり）、「先行き」は▲41％pt と更なる低下が予想されている（巻末の雇用概
況①上段右）。特に、中小企業（全産業）は「最近」が▲42％pt と6 月調査から3％pt 低下し、
「先行き」は▲45％pt だった。中小企業を中心に、人手不足感が強まっているとみられる。 
他方、トランプ米政権による高関税政策（トランプ関税）1F
2や、長期化する日中関係の冷え込
みは、引き続き労働需要の下押し要因となるだろう。 
また、雇用環境の悪化リスクは小さくない。中東情勢は依然として不確実性が高い状況が続
いており、とりわけ注意が必要だ。原油等の供給網の正常化は当面見込みにくく、輸入原油価
格が高止まりしている。特に原油高は原材料費や物流費などの上昇を通じて企業収益を悪化さ
せ3、幅広い産業で労働需要を下押しする可能性がある。

1 日本労働組合総連合会（連合）が公表した第 7 回（最終）回答集計結果では、定期昇給相当込みの賃上げ率
（加重平均）が企業規模計で 5.01％と、前年（5.25％）をやや下回るものの、高水準を維持している。300 人
未満の中小組合では 4.69％と前年（4.65％）をわずかながら上回り、中小企業の積極的な姿勢が目立った。詳
細は、日本労働組合総連合会（連合）「3 年連続の 5％超え！～2026 春季生活闘争 第7 回（最終）回答集計結
果について～」（2026 年7 月3 日）を参照。 
2 トランプ米政権は通商法 122 条に基づく措置が失効する 7 月 24 日に新たな関税措置を発動した（日本経済新
聞 電子版「トランプ政権が新関税発動、日本 12.5％ 中間選挙へ看板政策継続」（2026 年7 月 24 日））。 
3 原油高による物価の上昇については、神田慶司・畑中宏仁・中村華奈子・横田凱「日本経済見通し：2026 年
8 月」（大和総研レポート、2026 年8 月 24 日）を参照。

Document 153: A reprieve, not a recovery, for EU chemical producers

Source PDF: all report pdf/A reprieve, not a recovery, for EU chemical producers.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 13
Status: ok

### Page 1

The Iran war has hit EU chemicals less severely than feared,
with production, capacity utilisation and margins improving in
recent months. But renewed import competition will cloud the
outlook once Gulf oil and gas supplies to Asia recover. To reverse
the industry's long-term decline, producers and policymakers
must accelerate the green transition
Edse Dantuma
Polyethylene, the world's most common plastic, is used to make everyday
products like packaging ﬁlm, shopping bags, and bottles

Boost from softer Asian competition
The Iran war has hit European chemical producers less severely
than feared. After the ﬁrst tariﬀ shock in early 2025, demand
initially rose as customers brought forward purchases amid supply
chain uncertainty. The main boost, however, came from weaker
imports. Damage to Middle Eastern plants and Strait of Hormuz
blockades sharply reduced production and exports. About a quarter
of regional polyethylene capacity was damaged, with repairs likely

to take months.

### Page 2

Elsewhere in Asia, producers faced prolonged feedstock and energy
shortages. Asia depends far more on Gulf oil, gas and raw materials
than the EU does. Before the conﬂict, Asian countries sourced 40-
90% of their oil from the region, compared with around 20% for the
EU.
Earnings improve strongly, but production
returns to previous level
Reduced competition and tighter supply conditions drove three
consecutive months of growth in EU chemical production from

February to April, while sharp price rises provided a signiﬁcant
boost to second-quarter earnings. But the respite proved short-lived
as output contracted again as inventory restocking faded and
transport links through the Gulf improved.
By June, EU chemical production was 0.5% lower than a year
earlier and remained around 24% below early-2022 levels.
Meanwhile, exceptionally low Rhine water levels continue to disrupt
raw-material shipments and production schedules, particularly in

Germany and the Netherlands.

Spain is Europe’s exception
Germany and the Netherlands have recorded the largest production
declines among the EU’s six biggest chemical producers. Together
with Belgium, they host Europe’s largest petrochemical and basic
chemicals cluster: the Antwerp-Rotterdam-Rhine-Ruhr Area. Highly
energy-intensive processes therefore weigh heavily on their
chemical sectors.
Spain stands out: chemical output has risen relatively strongly this

year and was the only top-six country to record growth in both
2024 and 2025. Its growth over the past decade, despite
ﬂuctuations, is also notable. Spain’s competitive position beneﬁts
from relatively low energy costs, a smaller share of basic chemicals,
a larger consumer chemicals segment, favourable domestic
demand and its location. A pipeline supplies Algerian gas, while EU
recovery funds have helped accelerate the expansion of renewable
power capacity.

Spain's chemical industry has held up
relatively well in recent years

### Page 4

*seasonally adjusted, 2-month moving average
Source: Eurostat
Temporary relief from competition and pressure on basic
chemicals
While the EU chemical industry has broadly been under pressure for
years, performance varies signiﬁcantly across segments. Four of
the ﬁve hardest-hit categories are in basic chemicals, which
produce base products and intermediates. Since early 2022, output
has fallen by between 25% in dyes and pigments and 47% in other
organic basic chemicals. The latter is particularly energy-intensive

and remains heavily reliant on fossil hydrocarbons for both energy
and feedstock. Compared with the previous downturn, basic
chemical producers beneﬁted from weaker international
competition in the ﬁrst half of this year. Only agricultural chemicals
fell sharply, as prolonged drought in the second quarter reduced
demand for crop protection products.
Consumer chemicals, including oils, extracts, perfumes and
toiletries, are the only segments to have grown both since early

2022 and since the start of this year. These downstream products
are less exposed to volatile energy prices and generate more value
through innovation. Speciality chemicals such as paints, coatings
and cleaning products sit between the two: output remains
structurally weak but has declined less than in basic chemicals.
Basic chemicals have been the hardest hit in
recent years

EU chemical activity follows the ebb and
ﬂow of the Iran war
The EU chemical production rebound was short-lived because of the
June US-Iran ceaseﬁre. Transport through the Strait of Hormuz
resumed and energy prices temporarily fell sharply. Chinese oil
reﬁning and exports of oil-based chemicals to the EU recovered. In
July, these exports were almost 40% higher than a year earlier,

while exports of organic basic chemicals – which account for about
two-thirds of China’s chemical exports to the EU – rose by nearly
50%.

Production expectations rise as Middle East conﬂict ﬂares up
again
Since the conﬂict intensiﬁed again in July, Gulf oil, gas and
feedstock shipments crucial to Asian chemical activity have fallen
sharply. Although the switch from oil to coal oﬀers a partial
alternative, this is expected to increase pressure on Asian chemical
production and exports again, which will provide some relief to
European producers.
In August and September, most EU producers therefore expected to

raise output, with expectations reaching a two-year high in
September. European chemicals will beneﬁt from weaker
competition while transport remains disrupted. However,
alternative Gulf routes will probably gain importance if the conﬂict
persists, eventually reviving Asian import competition. Oil
shipments through Hormuz have recently increased to almost 80%
of pre-war levels.

Source: European Commission
Speciality and consumer chemicals have the
strongest outlook
Order books improved slightly again after a brief dip in September.
Yet producers’ assessment of orders remains much further below its
long-term average (-26% versus -14%) than production
expectations, highlighting persistent structural weakness.
The historically wide gap between upstream and downstream
activities has narrowed this year, as have sentiment diﬀerences

between countries. Sentiment improved in basic-chemicals-heavy
Germany, Belgium and the Netherlands, but weakened in France,
Italy and Spain, where basic chemicals play a smaller role. This
reﬂects basic chemicals’ temporarily improved competitive position
but also continued weak demand.
European growth remains constrained by higher inﬂation and only
a limited recovery in end markets. Higher energy prices squeeze
consumer purchasing power, hitting consumer chemicals hardest.

Speciality chemicals have better prospects as investment rises and
the AI boom lifts demand for products and materials used in:
Sustainability and electriﬁcation, including battery materials,
chemical catalysts, ﬂame retardants, lightweight resins and
composites.
Defence equipment, including reinforced plastic ﬁbres,
advanced coatings and lubricants.

### Page 8

Semiconductors, including photosensitive polymers and ultra-

Higher energy and feedstock costs will bite
more gradually
Chemical prices closely track energy costs, so expensive and
volatile energy will also make them higher and more volatile. Yet
the steepest rise in selling prices appears to be over. Although most
producers expected to raise prices in August and September, the
expectations index was stable just below its long-term average – a

far milder increase than after the Iran war began. This suggests
supply is gradually expanding again and price competition is
increasing.
Greater diﬃculty passing on input-cost increases will put margins
under growing pressure in the second half of the year, reinforced as
higher energy costs feed through into purchase prices. Layered
hedging – through long-term contracts, futures and other
instruments – spreads this impact over time and helped margins

improve sharply in the ﬁrst half. Large chemical companies in
particular use rolling hedging strategies, ﬁxing or mitigating prices
for diﬀerent periods: from several months to several years,
depending on the product, company circumstances and market
outlook.
Selling price expectations stayed benign in
September
Selling price expectations for the months ahead, EU chemical industry

### Page 9

Source: European Commission
Structural problems will resurface
The European chemical industry’s structural problems are far from
over. Demand has been weak for years, while competitiveness
continues to erode. European energy prices are substantially higher
than in the US and China, and government support is much lower
than in China. Once transport ﬂows normalise, global overcapacity
will again drive an inﬂux of cheap imports, especially from China.

Global chemical capacity grew by about 15% between 2018 and
2023 and, based on projects already underway, is expected to rise
by another 10% through 2028, keeping structural overcapacity in
place. Over the past decade, the EU’s share of global chemical sales
halved to 13%, while China’s nearly quintupled to 46%. The EU’s
chemicals trade deﬁcit with China widened from €21bn in 2023 to
€24bn in 2025. An artiﬁcially weak Chinese yuan and US import
tariﬀs further undermine Europe’s competitiveness.

Restructuring wave is not over
Despite somewhat better market conditions this year, restructuring
is likely to continue. Most major players have announced strategic
interventions in recent years. Earlier this year, SABIC sold its
European petrochemical operations, while Evonik is implementing a
multi-year plan of divestments, closures and reorganisations. Ineos
plans to close three UK chemical plants, and Shell wants to sell its
entire chemicals division, including a major European cluster.

European chemical capacity shrank by 30 million tonnes, or 7%,
between 2022 and 2026. Announced plant closures increased
sixfold over this period, while investment fell sharply. Petrochemical

Climate costs pose a growing challenge
Climate costs also put Europe at a competitive disadvantage to
countries without carbon pricing or with much lower pricing. As free

allowances are phased out, EU ETS costs will probably weigh
increasingly on production costs towards 2030, both directly
through companies’ own emissions and indirectly through higher
electricity prices. In July 2026, the European Commission proposed
extending the emissions-reduction pathway, modestly easing the
cost increase, alongside more ﬁnancial support for clean-
technology investment.
CBAM oﬀers only partial protection for EU chemicals
Fertilisers and hydrogen are covered by the CBAM levy, which

protects against imports not subject to carbon costs. In
return, free allowances for CBAM products will be phased out

faster, reaching zero by 2034. This will widen the cost gap
between eﬃcient, electriﬁed installations and older fossil-
based plants. Besides the faster phase-out, CBAM has several
drawbacks for chemical companies:
The levy can be circumvented when a chemical feedstock
is covered by CBAM but a product containing it is not.
This may encourage imports of ﬁnished or intermediate
products from outside the EU. The European
Commission’s proposed expansion mainly targets

downstream steel and aluminium products, not the
wider chemical value chain.
Most chemicals remain outside CBAM. Chemical value
chains involve many processes, intermediates and
feedstocks, making embedded emissions diﬃcult to
calculate. Organic chemicals have therefore been
excluded for now.
CBAM does not oﬀset the EU ETS-related competitive
disadvantage faced by EU companies in export markets.
Promising decarbonisation projects remain operationally

challenging
To regain competitiveness, European plants must decarbonise
faster through carbon capture and storage, process electriﬁcation,
and recycled and bio-based feedstocks. Although projects are
regularly cancelled, promising initiatives remain. INEOS plans to
start Project One in Antwerp this year, introducing a modern
ethylene cracker that initially emits less than half as much CO ₂  as
the EU’s most eﬃcient cracker and becomes climate-neutral within

ten years.
Spain’s Repsol aims to decarbonise its Tarragona complex through
circular chemicals and methanol, large-scale green hydrogen and
oﬀshore CO ₂  storage. In September, fertiliser producer Yara began
shipping liquid CO ₂  from Sluiskil in the Netherlands to Norway for
the permanent underground storage of 800,000 tonnes of CO ₂  a

Such projects can quickly cost hundreds of millions of euros a year.

The €5bn Project One has contributed to the doubling of INEOS’s
debt since 2021, while the group suﬀered a loss of more than
€500m in 2025. Chemicals are therefore widely seen as a hard-to-
abate sector. Governments often co-invest, but poor operating
conditions, inadequate green infrastructure and weak demand for
sustainable products still obstruct large-scale decarbonisation.
Infrastructure and decarbonisation
investment are essential
Through its European Chemicals Industry Action Plan, the European

Commission aims to prevent essential chemical production from
leaving the EU. The European Critical Chemicals Alliance brings
policymakers and producers together to identify, monitor, protect
and support critical European production. Industry supports the EU
ETS goals and system, but argues that suﬃcient free allowances
should remain until eﬀective protection against carbon leakage and
adequate demand for sustainable products are in place.
This requires supportive EU policy. Europe’s chemical

transformation is becoming a race against time: more plants will
inevitably close, leaving only the most eﬃcient and fastest-
decarbonising companies and clusters. Brussels’ growing focus on
incentives and safeguards against unwanted international
dependencies therefore oﬀers the sector welcome support.

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
ECONOMIC INSIGHT
As was universally expected, the RBA Monetary Policy
Board (MPB) lifted the cash rate by 25bps to 4.6% at its
September 2026 meeting.
This is quite the turnaround from the 11 August meeting,
where an extended period on hold looked to be the base case.

At that time, the data had broken against the RBA’s more
hawkish view, and its published forecasts did not support
further hikes. But the MPB can now point to the emergence of
some of the upside risks to inflation it warned of in August.
The post-meeting statement was notable for its emphasis
on the Middle East conflict, noting that “global energy prices
are now much higher than had been assumed in the August
forecasts” – “much higher” is stronger language than one

normally sees in an RBA statement. The RBA’s liaison indicates
that firms are passing this on or planning to do so. The pass-
through was characterised as partial, and additional to
domestically driven inflation arising from capacity pressures. We
read this as implying that the RBA is concerned about upside
risks to underlying inflation in Q4 of this year, as well as Q3.
The RBA is also wary about pressure on both construction
costs and retail prices of IT goods arising from the AI/data

centre boom, although in the media conference, the Governor
clarified that risks there were building rather than materialising.
The broader background highlighted in the Governor’s opening
statement to the media conference, is of demand outstripping
supply. The statement evidenced this by noting the stronger
July CPI and June quarter GDP results than expected.
The accompanying statement was notable for the absence of
language about the labour market being a little tight, despite

this being the inter-meeting message from the Governor
and other staff (see, for example, the Governor’s opening
statement on 18 September) and the language in the post-
meeting media conference. Views clearly differ on this: we
note that the Treasury forecasts underlying the May budget
and the recent Intergenerational Review imply both more
spare capacity in the labour market (a lower NAIRU) and faster
growth in potential from wherever the starting point is (higher

productivity growth and trend increase in participation).
Instead, the post-meeting statement characterised the
labour market as easing “broadly as expected”, despite the
unemployment rate already rising higher than the August SMP
forecast of 4.5% for the December quarter.
RBA hikes in September, November follow-up now expected
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group
The decision was unanimous, suggesting that any qualms
external MPB members might have had about the RBA’s

downbeat view of supply capacity were overruled by resurgent
oil prices and the stronger than forecast CPI and GDP data.
With upside risks being seen to have emerged, the post-meeting
statement language included that the Board “will continue to
do what it considers necessary to bring inflation sustainably
back to target, including increasing the cash rate target further if
needed”, a small but meaningful change from the “if upside risks
materialise” language from the previous meeting, and back to

the tone of the language at the June meeting.
The bar for a follow-up hike in November is low. Indeed,
judging by the post-meeting rhetoric, a November hike is now
the base case, absent a lasting resolution of the Middle East
conflict beforehand, or some other event that significantly
lowers the outlook for energy-related costs in Australia. The
bar for hikes beyond that is much higher, given the cumulative
rise in interest rates, and noting that the labour market is

easing and the housing market will likely weaken further.
The August inflation data released the day after the meeting
was in line with our expectations. We therefore continue to
expect Q3 trimmed mean inflation around 0.9%qtr, too high for
the RBA’s comfort, and upside risks for Q4. Likewise, there are
no implications for our rates view from this week’s inflation data.
Forecast changes
• We have updated our financial forecasts to reflect the change

in our rate view.
• Our labour market forecasts are unchanged on the first
decimal place, but there is some modest upside risk to our
unemployment forecast.
• Our updated GDP profile can be found on page 9.

3
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
THE WEEK THAT WAS
Cliff Notes: searching for signal
led the correction to date, the fact that the other capitals
are also reporting price declines clearly indicates that the
correction is deepening and broadening, as higher interest
rates collide with the tax policy changes announced in May’s

budget. Sentiment effects indeed remain at play, as evinced
by the continued pull-back in investor credit growth . Westpac
anticipates a peak-to-trough decline of around 7%, with tight
on-market supply limiting the extent of the correction. For
more, see our latest Housing Pulse.
Offshore, there was plenty of data to dissect.
In the US, inflation eased in August despite ongoing strength
in household demand and higher energy prices. Headline PCE
inflation rose 0.3%mth and 3.4%yr, while core PCE increased

0.2%mth and 3.0%yr. Encouragingly for the FOMC, the
share of PCE components rising by more than 3% over the
past year, a measure frequently cited by Fed Chairman Kevin
Warsh, declined to 45% from 49%, suggesting some easing in
broad-based inflation pressures. At the same time, household
spending remained robust, increasing 0.9%mth, equivalent
to around 0.6%mth in real terms. That said, personal income
growth was soft, rising 0.2% in the month, signalling

households opted to draw down savings.
The ISM manufacturing survey was stronger than the
headline suggested. While the index edged down 0.1pt to
54.5, manufacturing has now expanded for nine consecutive
months. There were some signs of capacity constraints
emerging, with production softened in the month but backlogs
up 4.6pts to 56.4, suggesting that firms struggled to keep
pace with demand. New orders rose 1.6pts to 55.3, primarily
due to domestic demand as new export orders eased, backlogs

increased. Firms responded by lifting employment, 1.5pts to
52.7. Against this backdrop manufacturers saw a renewed
acceleration in cost pressures, with the prices paid index
jumping 6.8pts to 77.9. Firms cited higher steel and aluminium
prices, elevated energy costs, rising freight charges and
ongoing semiconductor shortages, with every major industry
reporting higher input costs during the month.
The past week’s data will have done nothing to bridge the

clear divide across the FOMC, as evident in this week’s busy
Fedspeak. Some participants argued for further rate hikes,
citing strong inflation, diminished supply capacity and policy
that is not yet restrictive. Others counselled patience, pointing
to progress on inflation and that September’s hike buys time
to assess upcoming data. The one thing both agree on is that
inflation will be the primary determinant of the timing and
extent of further rate hikes. Our view remains that one further

hike is likely this cycle to bring policy closer to neutral.
In Australia, this week’s main event was the RBA Monetary
Policy Board’s (MPB) decision to raise the cash rate by 25bps
to 4.60%. This outcome was universally expected given
Governor Bullock had already communicated that upside risks
to inflation had materialised – the conditionality established
by the MPB back in August for considering another rate hike.
Governor Bullock made explicit note of three upside risks.

The Middle East conflict was emphasised as the risk that had
“materialised”, given the re-escalation and renewed surge in crude
oil and refined product prices over recent months. The RBA’s
liaison warns this could lead to further pass-through of costs
and more persistent inflation pressures. On top of this, domestic
capacity pressures and the data centre/AI investment boom were
also noted as upside risks that the MPB remain alert to.
In a video update mid-week, Chief Economist Luci Ellis

explains why the bar for a follow-up rate hike in November is
low. We now expect a 25bp increase in November, provided
there is no sudden resolution of the Middle East conflict
beforehand, or some other event that significantly lowers the
outlook for energy-related costs.
Subsequently, the August CPI revealed that underlying
(trimmed mean) inflation rose 0.2% (3.4%yr), which was
slightly below the market’s expectation but spot on Westpac’s
forecast. As usual, the monthly detail was mixed, but the key

drivers of underlying inflation remain centred on housing-
related components, particularly rents and new dwelling
purchases. Headline inflation remained volatile, with the
unwind of the fuel excise cut causing another spike in fuel
prices. We have maintained our forecast for a 0.9% lift in
trimmed mean inflation in Q3, and with upside risks building
for Q4, a follow-up rate hike in November appears most likely.
Regarding the current growth pulse, household spending

surprised with a flat reading in August. Services spend fell back
following recent strength that was driven by major sporting
events, but this was offset by an increase in goods spend,
particularly in fuel and electric vehicles. Together with our
latest card data for early September, the consumer spending
pulse is shading a touch softer. However, another solid
outcome for business investment could once again be on the
cards for Q3. August’s goods trade data showcased another

significant spike in data centre-related equipment imports, up
$4.3bn, similar in scale to Q1. While clearly import-driven, our
modelling suggests that the local economic impact of such
investment is likely to be around half of the total investment.
Before moving offshore, a final note on housing. Cotality’s
latest update revealed that dwelling prices fell another 1.1%
nationally in September. While Sydney and Melbourne have
Sian Fenner, Head of Business and Industry Economics

Ryan Wells, Economist
Illiana Jain, Economist

4
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
In China, policymakers announced another round of targeted
support measures aimed at stabilising growth and supporting
the struggling property sector. The PBOC lowered rates on its
Pledged Supplementary Lending facility by 25bps to 1.5% and
expanded quotas for targeted lending programs. Authorities

also introduced subsidies for eligible first-home buyers. The
subsidy applies to homes with a floor area of 120m² or less
and a value below CNY1.5mn, with support capped at 1ppt of
mortgage costs, up to CNY10,000 and for a maximum term of
five years. The measures complement reforms announced in
August to improve developer financing conditions and support
housing completion. However, weak credit demand and
confidence remains a significant constraint, suggesting policy

transmission is still limited.
The September PMI data pointed to a modest improvement
in activity, although the details were less convincing.
Manufacturing returned to expansionary territory, rising 0.9pts
to 50.1, supported by stronger production and new orders.
Much of the improvement, however, reflected a rebound in
prices, with the raw materials price index rising 7.6pts and
producer prices increasing 6.2pts. Labour market conditions
remained weak, with the employment index falling a further

0.6pts and remaining below 50 for almost four years. Conditions
in the non-manufacturing sector also improved, with the
services PMI lifting from 49.0 to 50.2, driven largely by stronger
construction activity. Input and output prices moved higher,
selling prices returned back above 50, export orders improved
to 48.5 and employment increased 0.5pts, although at 45.9 it
remains firmly in contractionary territory. Overall, the surveys
suggest activity is stabilising and policy support is gaining some

traction, but weak labour demand, subdued credit growth and
soft domestic demand indicate the recovery remains fragile.
In Japan, the September Tankan survey delivered another
constructive signal on the economy and reinforced the case
for further policy normalisation. Business conditions improved
by 2pts for large manufacturers and 5pts more broadly, while
profitability indicators strengthened and capital expenditure
intentions remained elevated. Large manufacturers expect

fixed investment to increase by 11.6% in FY2026, while large
non-manufacturers expect investment growth of 11.2%,
pointing to continued confidence in the medium-term outlook.
Capacity indicators improved further across both
manufacturing and non-manufacturing sectors, with measures
of excess production capacity becoming less negative
and indicating that demand remains sufficiently strong to
keep pressure on existing capacity. Labour shortages also

intensified, with employment conditions becoming more
negative across most industries and firm sizes, pointing to a
progressively tighter labour market. Inflation expectations
remained anchored around 2%, providing further evidence
that Japan is moving towards a more sustainable inflation
environment. Overall, the survey was consistent with an
economy operating close to capacity and supports the view
that a December rate hike remains a realistic possibility.

### Page 5

5
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FOCUS ON NEW ZEALAND
Resilient activity and simmering inflation
While bumpy and uneven, New Zealand’s economic
recovery has continued over the past few months, with
activity showing resilience in the face of continued global
headwinds. Under the surface, however, inflation pressures

are bubbling away, with much of that pressure centred on
parts of the economy that aren’t sensitive to interest rates.
Economic activity getting back on firmer footing
Looking first at recent activity data, the economy has been
finding its feet again after taking a knock in the wake of the
Middle East war. That has been seen most clearly in the jobs
market, with the number of filled jobs rising by 0.9% in the
year to August. While not shooting the lights out, that is still a

noticeable improvement from the early part of the year, when
filled job numbers were falling.
Under the surface, it’s still a very mixed picture. Agriculture
remains the main engine of growth, with the strength in
commodity export earnings helping to boost incomes,
confidence and spending in regions with strong rural backbones.
That’s supported firm job growth in regions like Canterbury
(where filled job numbers are up 1.9% over the past year), Otago

(+1.5%) and Southland (+3.1%). In contrast, jobs growth remains
much more moderate in Auckland (up 0.6%) and Wellington
(up just 0.4%). That town-and-country split is likely to remain a
feature of the economic landscape for some time yet.
Looking ahead, businesses expect trading conditions to
remain resilient through the final months of the year. That
was reflected in September’s Business Outlook survey.
While the survey’s gauges of expected business activity and

general business confidence eased over the past month, they
remain at firm levels, consistent with continued moderate
growth through the latter part of the year. That resilience has
also been evident in the household sector, with consumer
confidence largely steady through September.
The resilience in household and business sentiment is notable,
as fuel prices have taken a big step higher over the past month.
The average price of 91 unleaded has risen by around 49c/litre –

albeit late in the month and likely not fully reflected in surveyed
confidence – taking it back to the levels seen during the early
stages of the Middle East conflict. Those higher fuel prices
have taken a bite out of household disposable incomes, with
spending on fuel up about 22% compared with this time last
year, even though the volume of fuel sold is down around 11%.
But even with the lift in fuel prices, we haven’t seen the same
degree of pessimism as earlier in the year, when fuel prices first

spiked. Notably, those higher fuel prices haven’t spilled over
into a broader lift in output price pressures either (at least at
this stage). Inflation expectations were steady in September’s
household and business surveys. Similarly, the number of firms
planning to raise prices fell back despite a lift in wage and cost
expectations. However, while that stability in pricing gauges is
welcome, measures of inflation pressure remain elevated.
Taken together, recent data reinforce indications that the

economy is shaking off the impacts of the Iran war.
Persistent inflation drivers and the RBNZ
Turning to inflation, the spike in oil prices earlier this year saw
annual inflation climb to 4.1% in the year to June. With local
fuel costs rising again in recent weeks, we continue to expect
inflation to remain above 3% until at least mid-2027.
However, even before the recent rise in fuel costs, inflation had
been running hot for an extended period. Excluding the rise

in food and fuel prices, inflation in the year to June was 2.9%,
and it’s been running close to or above 3% for five years now.
As we discuss in our recent report, much of the strength in
inflation over the past year has been related to administered
costs, such as local council rates, or to sectors that are less
exposed to competitive pressures, such as electricity and
insurance. Those increases have persisted for an extended period,
and we expect further sizeable increases in many of these costs.

This ongoing firmness is a key reason why we expect domestic
(non-tradables) inflation to be higher than the RBNZ expects
over the longer term. It’s also part of the reason why we
expect the RBNZ will ultimately need to raise the OCR by
more than it assumed in its September policy statement.
We are forecasting a 25bp rate rise at the RBNZ’s December
meeting and expect the OCR to peak at 4% next year.
This is a tough environment for the RBNZ. With ongoing

increases in administered prices boosting core inflation at a
time when energy prices are driving headline inflation well
above the target range, it’s hard for the RBNZ to look through
them. The RBNZ had little headroom to absorb upside inflation
surprises, even before the recent spike in oil prices.
Satish Ranchhod
Senior Economist

### Page 6

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
6
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
But getting inflation back to target in the face of an oil price
spike and high administered prices will not be easy and involves
some tough trade-offs. Administered prices tend to be less
responsive to changes in economic conditions. As a result,
interest rate hikes are likely to have a bigger impact on parts of

the economy that are not the main drivers of inflation. Tighter
policy will also weigh on the labour market and near-term
growth, while increasing households’ debt-servicing costs.
This is an uncomfortable but necessary trade-off. Looking
through, or not responding to, these sorts of large and persistent
cost increases would still leave households facing significant
increases in overall living costs. In the long run, it would be
more damaging for households and the economy if generalised

inflation were to become embedded in price setting behaviour.
The Kiwi has lost its wings
Adding to near-term inflation risks has been the drop in the NZ
dollar. On a trade-weighted basis, the Kiwi has fallen around
4% since the start of September, and it’s begun the December
quarter around 4% below the level the RBNZ assumed in its
September policy statement.
The slide in the Kiwi is mainly due to the strength of the US
dollar, with NZD/USD down nearly 5% since the start of

September to sit just below 0.56 at the time of writing. Moves
against other currencies have been more modest. For example,
NZD/AUD is down 2% over the same period.
Technically, NZD/USD seems oversold. In the short term,
trends in the US dollar will dominate, including any response
to the US labour market report due on Friday evening. Any
turnaround in the USD would likely see NZD/USD trade back
towards the top of the 0.5580–0.5680 range that is governing

the pair for the time being.
NZD/AUD will continue to be driven by changing views on
interest rate differentials between the RBNZ and RBA. This
week, NZD/AUD corrected higher from around 0.8070 to as high
as 0.8110 as RBA cash rate expectations were repriced following
the RBA Board meeting and Australian CPI. In the short term, the
bias for NZD/AUD could be lower, given our view that the RBNZ
will pause at its late-October meeting and that the market is

under-pricing the risk of an RBA rate hike in November.
Next week’s QSBO, due on Tuesday, will be the key event
risk, with the inflation and growth gauges closely watched for
signs that might prompt the RBNZ to pause or hike. Markets
had previously priced in a high chance of a hike at the RBNZ’s
October meeting, but in recent days appear to be reassessing
that view.  Pricing for a 25bp hike in New Zealand could be
pared back towards a 50:50 call. The 0.8030–0.8150 range

is likely to contain the pair in the immediate period ahead.
Our medium-term view is for NZD/AUD to rise as the RBNZ
increases the OCR to 4% by the second half of 2027. We’ll
update our views on the NZD and other currencies in our next
Market Outlook, out Wednesday 14 October.
Fiscal update
The big uncertainty on the domestic horizon is, of course,
November’s general election, and at this stage the polling
looks tight. Neither the established left nor right bloc has a

clear majority.
Ahead of the election, the Treasury has released its Pre-election
Economic and Fiscal Update (PREFU), updating the Budget
2026 (BEFU) fiscal forecasts to reflect the latest economic and
fiscal information and a revised economic outlook. This allows
the public to assess the state of the Government’s finances. It
also provides other parties with a baseline against which to
calibrate their spending and taxation policies.
The PREFU revealed a stronger starting point for the

Government’s finances than had been expected in the Budget.
• A deficit of $8.6bn is estimated for 2025/26, down from the
Budget estimate of $11.9bn.
• Similarly, the forecast deficit for 2026/27 has been revised
down significantly to $6.8bn from $11.4bn.
• Thereafter, the OBEGALx deficit is expected to continue
declining gradually, moving to a surplus of $4bn in 2028/29.
This broadly follows the Budget profile, but is stronger
across the forecast period.

• Net core Crown debt is forecast to peak at 43.9% of GDP in
2027/28, below the 46.1% peak forecast in the BEFU. It’s then
forecast to decline to 39.5% of GDP by the end of the forecast
period in 2030/31, in line with the Government’s goal of
reducing debt below 40% of GDP over the medium term.
Given the improved outlook, NZDM announced a $4bn
reduction in the 2026/27 bond programme to $30bn and a
$15bn reduction in the forecast programme across the whole

period through to 2029/30.
The medium-term forecasts in the PREFU should be treated
with caution. The economic forecasts underpinning the
PREFU were finalised on 21 August, when both oil prices
and, especially, bond yields were materially lower than they
are today. Had the forecasts been finalised more recently, the
fiscal projections presented today would have been weaker,
at least in the near term. Indeed, the Treasury is upfront in
acknowledging that, while the risks are two-sided, the balance

is skewed to the downside.
Aside from the obvious uncertainty surrounding the economic
outlook because of unpredictable developments in the Middle
East, the approaching General Election, and the possibility of
a change in the composition of the Government, means the
assumptions underpinning the PREFU forecasts could quickly
become invalidated.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUS: Oct Westpac-MI Consumer Sentiment (index)
Oct 6, Last: 84.4
Consumer sentiment declined 5.2% to 84.4 in September,
taking the index back towards the deeply pessimistic levels seen
earlier in the year. Rising fuel prices and fears of an imminent

interest rate rise drove the move with a continued weakening in
housing markets likely adding to unease in some quarters.
Developments over the last month would have added to these
concerns with: the RBA raising the cash rate by 25bps to 4.6%
during the survey week; average pump prices pushing above
$2.36/litre nationally and no end in sight to global supply
disruptions;  and more weak updates on the housing market.
NZ: Q2 NZIER Survey of Business Opinion (index)

Oct 6, General business confidence last: 12.3
Business conditions picked up modestly in the June quarter as
concerns about the war in the Middle East eased. However,
cost and pricing gauges remained firm. The September quarter
survey will be closely watched ahead of the RBNZ’s 28
October meeting. Recent weeks have seen signs that activity
is firming. However, fuel prices have also risen sharply, and we
(along with the RBNZ) will be watching for signs of a broader

lift in inflation pressures. The timing of survey responses
within the sample period will also warrant scrutiny, given that
oil prices rose late in the quarter.

Document 187: Australia’s Trade in Goods Account, August (2026-10-10)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Australia’s Trade in Goods Account, August (2026-10-10).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-10-10
Pages: 13
Status: ok

### Page 1

#trade #australia
Australia’s Trade in Goods Account, August
By Ryan Wells
Economist, Westpac
13:19 October 01 2026 Share
Another surge in data centre-related equipment imports in August, alongside continued
volatility in gold trade. Trade balance: $0.5bn surplus. Export values: 3.7%mth. Import
values: 5.8%mth.

### Page 2

Australia’s goods trade surplus narrowed to $0.5bn in August from $1.4bn in July (revised down from
$1.9bn previously). This result was significantly lower than expected – the market consensus was for a
broadly stable reading, while Westpac had a slight widening pencilled in. Over the past six months or
so, the trade balance has been oscillating between small deficits and surpluses, largely coinciding with
significant month-to-month volatility in the trade mix. The chief culprit behind this volatility has been

trade in non-monetary gold, among other components.
Before discussing the detail, it is once again worth noting the results for gold up front. In August, non-
monetary gold exports jumped 20%, while imports surged 91%. Excluding gold, the trade figures have
been much more stable, closer to a deficit of $2-3bn per month over the past three months, down from
larger deficits around the start of the year.
Export earnings rose 3.7% in August, bouncing back from a 3.6% decline in July. The trend for exports

so far this year looks a bit more positive, but again, this is tricky to read given the volatility present in
the data. Excluding gold, export earnings were up 1.6% in August. This was largely supported by a
4.7% lift in coal and a 2.8% increase in LNG, with further detail provided by the ABS suggests that an
increase in both volumes and prices contributed. However, iron ore (–1.5%) and rural goods (–1.0%)
weighed on earnings in the month.

### Page 3

The import bill meanwhile increase by 5.8% in August. Once again, a surge in automatic data
processing (ADP) equipment imports – which capture the investment upswing related to data centres –
were in the spotlight, surging 79% in the month up to $4.3bn. There were also an increase in other
capital goods imports centred on telecommunications equipment (+12%) and civil aircraft (+30%) –
large in their own right but dwarfed by the surge in ADP imports in the month.

Our latest estimates have the total pipeline for data centres at around $175bn over the coming decade,
with much of that being front-loaded. It is natural to expect month-to-month volatility in ADP equipment
imports as these large and lumpy data centre projects are worked through, but it is worth highlighting
that August’s jump closely resembles the surge from March, when imports also surged to around
$4.3bn. Given the scale of the increase in August, Q3 is shaping up to be another quarter where data

centre investment will play a significant role in driving growth – while it is true that the data centre build-
out is import-intensive, our modelling suggests that once spending enters Australia, the local economic
impact is likely to be around half of the total investment.

### Page 4

Elsewhere across the import basket, while gold imports also surged, it was effectively offset by a
decline in consumer imports of non-industrial transport equipment (i.e. cars), down 21.2% in August
following the surge from May to July. While still tentative at this stage, it suggests that the momentum in
EV purchases, which played a significant role in the consumer spending story in Q2, may be starting to
wane. Additionally, a further pull-back in fuel imports (–5.0%) in August has extended the downward

trend in recent months, following the initial surge in oil prices in March and April after the war in the
Middle East began. However, with the conflict re-escalating recently and margins on refined fuel
products widening, we could see this trend reverse over the coming months.

Document 198: Capital Market Outlook (2026-09-28)

CHIEF INVESTMENT OFFICE
Capital Market Outlook

2 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
The Five Shocks of 2026—And the Extraordinary Resilience of the U.S. Economy
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
Lauren Sanfilippo, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
This is the year the consensus got mugged.
Rewind to the start of the year. The market consensus was that the U.S. economy was
set to slow. The Fed was set to cut interest rates. Corporate earnings were set to grow—

but not explode. Energy prices would remain tame. And the odds looked good that the
Republicans would control the House, the Senate, or both, following the midterm election.
Nine months on, the landscape looks starkly different. Defined by five shocks—energy,
interest rates, earnings, growth, and politics—2026 looks nothing like the year investors
thought they were buying on January 1.
Shock 1: Diesel Matters. While global oil prices have moved higher since the start of

the U.S./Iran war on February 28, oil prices have yet to surpass their previous record high
because the world has released some 400 million barrels from strategic reserves this year,
China has curbed its demand for oil imports, and the U.S. has goosed its energy exports to
record highs.
But here is the problem: While gasoline powers the American consumer, diesel powers the
American economy—and diesel prices are now sitting at record highs. Diesel supplies have

been hammered by wars in the Middle East and Ukraine that have sharply reduced refining
capacity. According to the International Energy Agency, by August, exports of diesel from
refiners in the Middle East had fallen by a quarter from prewar levels. Meanwhile, Ukraine
has hit 24 of Russia’s 34 largest refineries, reducing supplies from one of the world’s
largest diesel exporters. The upshot: U.S. diesel prices have soared from $3.55 a gallon
at the start of the year to roughly $6.52 a gallon. While crude oil trades for around $100

a barrel, the retail equivalent of a barrel of diesel now costs more than $260. The diesel
shock of 2026 was the last thing investors saw coming—and remains a key impediment to
structurally lower inflation.
Shock 2: From Interest Rate Cuts to Rate Hikes. Perhaps no other consensus view
entering 2026 has been turned more dramatically on its head than expectations for the
Fed. Heading into 2026, markets were pricing in rate cuts on expectations of a cooling

labor market, moderating inflation, and slower economic growth. The Fed easing cycle was
expected to continue in 2026. In January 2026, futures showed a roughly 50% chance of a
cut by March.
Expectations, however, flipped in late February with the start of the U.S./Iran war. The
Middle East energy shock reignited inflationary pressures, which, combined with stronger-
than-expected U.S. economic growth and a tightening labor market, have demolished the

odds of easier money in 2026. Short- and long-term Treasury yields rose sharply last week
in the wake of the S&P Global Flash U.S. Composite Purchasing Managers’ Index (PMI)
which pointed to the strongest business expansion in more than five years and the fastest
employment growth in over four years, while also reinforcing expectations that the Fed
will maintain a restrictive policy stance for longer. Markets were expecting two to three
quarter-point rate cuts in 2026; instead, the Fed has already raised rates once this year and

is expected to keep raising rates over the near term.
Shock 3: The Earnings Boom No One Saw Coming. Not all the shocks of 2026 are
negative. Indeed, stronger-than-expected corporate earnings have been among the most
positive factors of this year. Corporate America entered the year facing questions about
tariffs, AI-related capital expenditure spending, the health of the U.S. consumer, operating
leverage and the U.S. dollar outlook. At the start of the year, Wall Street was expecting

roughly $290 of S&P 500 earnings per share for 2026, per Bloomberg estimates. But by
September 21, the estimate had risen by $50, to $340 per share.
Investment Implications
While some of these shocks may
contribute to periods of market
volatility, the combination of strong
earnings growth, robust domestic
demand, and ongoing capital
investment suggests that the
fundamental backdrop for Equities
remains constructive.

### Page 3

3 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
Earnings have been upgraded and underpinned by several factors, including robust final 
domestic demand in the U.S., a pickup in global growth, AI-related capital spending, 
productivity gains, and enhanced operating leverage. All these variables have helped deliver 
an earnings “shock” to Wall Street. And as an aside, yes, headline profit growth has been 
inflated by strong technology earnings and one-off investment gains from Amazon and 
Alphabet. That said, the S&P 500’s earnings story remains extraordinary. According to 
FactSet, the 493 companies excluding the Magnificent 7 1 
posted 31.8% earnings growth in 
Q2, the strongest level since late 2021.

Shock 4: An Economy with more Horsepower than the Headlines Suggest. That 
corporate profits have exceeded expectations this ye
ar is due in no small part to an economy 
that continues to surprise to the upside. The Fed was supposed to cut rates this year to 
keep the economy humming and prevent it from losing steam. But rather than limp through 
2026, the economy has leaped ahead. Yes, real gross domestic product (GDP) growth of 
2.1% in Q1 and 1.5% in Q2 are hardly blockbuster annualized rates of growth. However, the 
figures belie the underlying strength of the U.S. economy, with real final domestic demand

(which strips out trade) running at a 4.2% annual rate in Q2. According to the latest reading 
of the Atlanta Fed GDP tracker, Q3 real GDP grow
th is running at a red-hot rate of 5.1%. 
Slowdown? What slowdown?
Resilient consumer spending, robust capital investment, record leve
ls of new business 
formation, and strong foreign direct investment inflows have combined to stoke growth 
in the $32 trillion behemoth called the U.S. economy—the most resilient and dynamic 
economy in the world.
Shock 5: The Unexpected Washington Reset. The last shock is still in the making, but 
given current trends, the odds are rising that the Democrats could control both the House 
and Senate follow
ing the midterm elections. To this point, in January, and per Kalshi, the 
Democrats had a 71% shot at the House and an 18% chance of taking the Senate. Fast-
forward to today, and the odds are a 90% chance that the Democrats take the House and a 
60% chance that they take the Senate.
How all of this plays out remains to be seen—there are some 36 days before the November 
vote, and anything can happen. The market base case remains that the Democrats take the 
House, and the Senate is a toss-up—or too close to call. But with the Democrats expected to 
control at least one chamber of Congress, inv
estors can expect more volatility and uncertainty 
about negotiations ov
er the budget, debt ceiling, defense spending and AI regulation. Another 
political wildcard to watch: the changing dynamics within the Democratic Party.
The Biggest Shock of All for 2026? Pe
rhaps it’s not $6 diesel, rising interest rates, 
solid growth, booming profits or shifting politics. Rather, it’s the ability of the U.S. 
economy to absorb the unexpected, not buckle under stress, and continue generating  wealth 
for investors. To wit, the S&P 500 is on pace for its fourth
 consecutive year of double-digit 
returns.

4 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
2026 Has, So Far, Defied the Traditional Midterm Year Playbook
Jordy Fuentes, Assistant Vice President and Investment Strategist
Current Landscape. To no surprise, market volatility persisted in 2026. Performance initially
tracked historical midterm election patterns before diverging meaningfully as the year
progressed, though there are still three months to go. Several items remain at hand as U.S.

voters head to the polls in November, including geopolitical and foreign policy uncertainty,
energy driven inflation pressures, affordability concerns, U.S. fiscal trajectory, and rapid
advancements in AI. Regardless of the eventual political outcome, history suggests that
markets and the economy can perform well across different configurations of government.
In our view, markets continue to be anchored by strong fundamentals and encouraging
seasonal trends.

To Be Addressed. U.S. domestic and foreign policy remain in focus as the global reorder,
tit-for-tat tariffs, and geopolitical conflicts spanning the Middle East to Eastern Europe have
added layers of uncertainty to the U.S. economic outlook.
At the same time, persistent geopolitical flare-ups have pushed oil prices materially
higher, adding to inflation pressures and keeping them above the Fed’s 2% target.
Headline Consumer Price Index (CPI) held above 3% year-over-year (YoY) in August, while

its core measure, which excludes food and energy, held at 2.4% YoY. The latest Personal
Consumption Expenditure (PCE), the Fed’s preferred measure of inflation, also remained
above 3% YoY for both its headline and core figures. 2
The U.S. fiscal trajectory is another area of focus. The U.S. budget deficit stands at 5.4% of
GDP , just above this century’s average of 4.6%. Meanwhile, U.S. public sector debt surpassed
the $40 trillion mark for the first time in August, oscillating around 100% of GDP .3 While these

trends warrant close monitoring, we view them as a slow-moving structural headwind rather
than an immediate concern, particularly within a highly innovative and diverse U.S. economy.
AI has emerged as a policy consideration, with debates increasingly centered on both
regulatory safeguards and the economic implications of the expanding infrastructure buildout.
For voters, those concerns extend to affordability, as the significant energy requirements of

new data centers raise questions about potential pressure on electricity costs.
Strap In for The Ride. Leading up to the midterm election, markets tend to experience
greater swings as investors navigate uncertainty surrounding the policy path for the
remainder of the presidential cycle (Exhibit 2A). Since 1930, the S&P 500 has averaged an
intrayear drawdown of 20% in midterm years, with the latest election in 2022 experiencing
a 25% intrayear pullback. This year, however, the S&P 500 largest peak-to-trough drawdown

has been 9%, considerably shallower than its long-term average (Exhibit 2B). Yet while
volatility may rise as election day approaches, 2026 has increasingly defied the traditional
midterm year playbook.
Why 2026 is Different. Despite entering a challenging stretch of the presidential cycle,
U.S. Equities have materially outpaced the typical year two pattern (Exhibit 2C). The S&P
500 experienced its best Q2 in a midterm year since 1938, surging 14.9% compared to an

average decline of 1.6%. From a monthly perspective, September stands out as the weakest
month, though month-to-date performance points to an unusual 1.0% rise. That resilience
follows a 2.6% gain in August, another month in which performance meaningfully exceeded
the historical decline of 0.5% (Exhibit 2D).
Through September, U.S. Equity resilience has also extended beyond headline returns with
both the frequency and magnitude of market pullbacks remaining relatively subdued. Market

corrections of 10% or more have occurred in just 29% of midterm election years since 1930.4
Against this backdrop, the market’s last correction came in spring 2025, while this year has
2 Bloomberg, Bureau of Labor Statistics. Data through August 31, 2026.
3 U.S. Treasury, Bureau of Economic Analysis. Data as of September 21, 2026.
4 Bloomberg, data through December 31, 2022.
Investment Implications
In our view, any related market
weakness could represent a

potential buying opportunity for
long-term investors.

### Page 5

5 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
seen only one 5%+ pullback compared to the average three per year, underscoring relatively
resilient market conditions.5 Year-to-date, the S&P 500 has increased 13%, well above the
average annual return of 3% according to Bloomberg through September 25, and on pace
to mark the strongest midterm election year since 2006. Taken together, 2026 has thus far
defied the traditionally weak midterm year seasonal pattern, reinforcing that every election

cycle is unique and historical trends may be a useful guide to navigate market volatility.
What’s Ahead? Forward performance is increasingly constructive for Equities. In the
post-war period, the S&P 500 rose 100% of the time in the six and 12 months following
elections, averaging 13% and 14%, respectively. 6 Even narrowing the time frame to three
months, markets ended higher 90% of the time. As investors push through the finish line,
the last three months of the year tend to be the strongest with October standing out,

ending higher 67% of the time. On average, Q4 has gained 5.6% since 1930, far surpassing
the preceding three quarters of election years. 7 Greater clarity about the U.S. fiscal policy
path serves as a potential tailwind for investors following the election.
To Take Home. Volatility tends to rise during midterm years, but history suggests that
reacting to short-term political uncertainty has rarely rewarded long-term investors. Over
longer horizons, the data suggests Equity returns and economic strength are shaped less

by election outcomes than by corporate profits, valuations, labor market conditions and
capital investments.

6 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THE TICKER TAPE
What is the bond market telling us?
Christopher Hyzy, Chief Investment Office
We believe the bond market is telling us that the Fed is on track to meeting their dual
mandate of price stability and full employment. More importantly, when examining the
full yield curve the 2-year Treasury yield is currently around 4.88%, which typically is
the most sensitive to the fed funds rate and tends to price in future potential policy

adjustments. At the same time, the 10-year Treasury yield is currently about 5.11%. This
leads us to believe that the market expects the Fed to engineer a couple of more hikes
while the back end of the curve is indicating that economic growth is still healthy.
Taken together, it appears the full curve, which has clearly flattened, believes the Fed is
doing its job, while supply shocks, for obvious reasons, are not leading to higher long-run
inflation. The backup in long-dated yields is growth, not necessarily inflation as indicated

by rising “real” yields. Therefore, we view the drift higher in yields as a more normal sign
than what many have originally suggested. We continue to emphasize higher economic
growth for longer, rising productivity in the medium term, supply shortages to remain in
key areas within the AI space, and shocks in the energy equation. We expect the energy
shocks to ultimately fade away over time, but we do not believe that growth fades
anytime soon. In our view, a more normal bond market pricing in Fed moves at the short

end, and growth at the long end helps financial conditions and economic growth operate
in a more durable manner.
In summary, the front-end of the curve expects 3 to 4 more hikes in the next 18 months
or so. This can be debated obviously based on future data especially short-term energy
supply shocks. Long-end yields are drifting higher based on five main factors below, in
our view.
1. Strong economic growth (both
services and manufacturing)
2. Hawkish Fed

3. Debt and deficit concerns
4. Lower demand for long duration
sovereign debt in general
5. Energy supply shocks
The largest weight being strong economic activity which kick starts portfolio
repositioning particularly as the Fed hikes rates. The repositioning stops once demand
rises again when yields reach attractive levels for the more patient longer term
investment community. The supply shocks ultimately end and debt and deficit worries will
likely remain, but they still take a backseat for now. Longer duration government debt is

becoming more attractive each week even in the face of the concerns above. Be patient

Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

S&P 500 Index Price Return is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States. A price return index is a stock
market index that tracks only the price changes and capital gains or losses of its component securities, without including dividends or cash distributions.
S&P Global Flash U.S. Composite Purchasing Managers’ Index is an early monthly economic indicator that estimates overall private-sector business activity by combining manufacturing

Investing involves risk, including the possible loss of principal. Past performance is no guarantee of future results.

or sale of any security or investment strategy. Merrill offers a broad range of brokerage, investment advisory and other services. There are important differences between brokerage and
investment advisory services, including the type of advice and assistance provided, the fees charged, and the rights and obligations of the parties. It is important to understand the differences,
particularly when determining which service or services to select. For more information about these services and their differences, speak with your Merrill financial advisor.

oversight and data analytics. CIO viewpoints are developed for Bank of America Private Bank, a division of Bank of America, N.A., (“Bank of America”) and Merrill Lynch, Pierce, Fenner & Smith

investment strategy and market views encompassing markets, economic indicators, asset classes and other market-related projections affecting GWIM.

of Bank of America Corporation.

of all investors.
Asset allocation, diversification and rebalancing do not ensure a profit or protect against loss in declining markets.
Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

Document 253: France’s National Rally fiscal plan faces major delivery risks

### Page 1

France’s National Rally, led by Marine Le Pen, is promising
€136bn in annual savings and aims to bring public spending
below 50% of GDP by 2032. Although the plan acknowledges
the need for ﬁscal consolidation, many of the projected savings
appear diﬃcult to deliver
Charlotte de Montpellier
The National Rally's counter budget acknowledges ﬁscal consolidation but
delivery may be diﬃcult
As discussed in our previous article, the government’s proposed

budget would limit the deterioration in the public ﬁnances in 2027,
but it would not stabilise the debt ratio or provide a lasting solution.
Attention is therefore increasingly turning to the ﬁscal policies that
could follow the presidential election. With the National Rally (RN)
currently well ahead in ﬁrst-round polls, its programme matters not
only as an opposition proposal, but as a possible framework for
policy after 2027.
Figures that are often difﬁcult to justify

### Page 2

The programme’s detailed breakdown is an improvement on
previous proposals and conﬁrms that the National Rally recognises
the need for ﬁscal consolidation. However, several measures lack a
clearly deﬁned base or mechanism, or depend on legal changes
and international agreements.
Fraud estimates illustrate the issue. The programme assigns €18bn
a year to a new VAT collection mechanism, although the total VAT
gap is estimated at €9bn to €15bn and includes errors, omissions

and bankruptcies, not only recoverable fraud. Other ﬁgures are
similarly uncertain. The plan projects €4.2bn in annual savings
from an unspeciﬁed reform of short-term Treasury bill
management and €4.4bn in motorway revenue from 2028,
although the main concessions expire only between 2031 and
2036 and an early buyout would cost an estimated €45bn to
€50bn.
State rationalisation is expected to yield almost €28bn from 2027,
but closing public bodies, reorganising departments and reducing

staﬀ take time and entail transition costs. The proposed cut in
France’s EU budget contribution – €11bn in 2027 and around €19bn
annually thereafter – could not be decided unilaterally. Changing
the EU’s own-resources system would require unanimous
agreement and ratiﬁcation by all member states.
The plan targets €37bn in social security savings, although the
detail and deliverability of this amount remain uncertain. Pensions
are another major uncertainty. The RN expects €15bn in savings

from tightening the contribution period required to validate a
quarter, which it says would oﬀset the €10bn cost of lowering the
retirement age to 60 or 62. Independent estimates, however, put
the cost of the age change alone at €25bn to €35bn per year.
Overall, some savings appear plausible, but not all of them and the
largest items are also the least well documented. This matters all
the more because the RN’s plan combines ﬁscal consolidation with

€69.3bn of tax cuts and new spending in 2027 alone. This includes
€53.2bn in revenue cuts, notably lower taxes on energy and
essential goods and the removal of several production taxes, as
